```json
    "originalFile": "\\FloatBarrier\n\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\subsection{Model Collapse: Theory and Empirical Evidence}\n\\label{sec:rw_collapse}\n\nIterative training collapse was formalized by \\citet{shumailov2024curse} and \\citet{alemohammad2024mad}; \\citet{schaeffer2025position} systematize eight distinct notions of collapse; our operationalization aligns with their \\emph{distributional shift} family (definitions~D4--D6).\nTheoretical analyses sharpen these results: \\citet{dohmatob2024tale} recast collapse as changed scaling-law exponents, \\citet{dohmatob2025strong} proved collapse at vanishingly small synthetic fractions, and \\citet{truong2026phase} prove entropy collapse is a first-order transition, structurally ruling out critical-slowing-down early-warning signals~\\citep{scheffer2009early,scheffer2012anticipating}.\nEmpirically, \\citet{hataya2023will} demonstrate that generated images degrade downstream classification on ImageNet-scale data, and \\citet{kovac2025recursive} show that training data properties (lexical diversity, semantic diversity) modulate the magnitude of recursive collapse in LLMs.\nCritical mixture ratios separating qualitative outcomes have independent precedent: \\citet{kazdan2025collapse} map collapse conditions, \\citet{gu2024cmr} establish scaling laws for critical ratios, and \\citet{gu2025datamixing} show data mixing induces phase transitions in knowledge acquisition.\n\n\\subsection{Detection of Synthetic Data Contamination}\n\\label{sec:rw_detection}\n\nMonitoring metrics proposed for collapse include expected calibration error (ECE)~\\citep{du2025scales}, surplexity~\\citep{gambetta2024surplexity}, and machine-generated text detection~\\citep{drayson2025detection}; \\citet{shi2025closer} independently validate entropy as a collapse diagnostic in diffusion models; \\citet{buffa2026entropy} propose continuous entropy-based monitoring for LLM accuracy, though targeting task performance rather than distributional collapse.\nA complementary line of work pursues \\emph{proactive} contamination prevention via watermarking~\\citep{kirchenbauer2023watermark}; our distributional detection framework is \\emph{reactive}---it identifies collapse from observed metric trajectories without requiring control over the generation process.\n\\citet{boettiger2012limits} establish fundamental power limits for early-warning detection in short time series ($T{<}25$), directly relevant to iterative training where observation windows are typically $T{=}5$--$20$.\nHowever, these works evaluate single metrics in isolation without cross-validating against alternative statistical tests.\nWe differ by applying five detection methods to identical data, mapping detectability across contamination regimes, and treating method sensitivity as a primary finding rather than a robustness check (detailed comparison: Appendix~\\ref{app:method_comparison}).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Methods for Distribution Shift}\n\\label{sec:rw_methods}\n\nFor temporal precedence testing, Granger causality~\\citep{granger1969investigating} is the standard framework, with neural extensions~\\citep{tank2021neural,runge2019detecting}; however, non-stationary series inflate rejection rates~\\citep{granger1974spurious}, and short series ($T{\\leq}25$) preclude reliable nonlinear estimation~\\citep{boettiger2012limits}.\nGranger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not true causation: bivariate tests cannot exclude confounders~\\citep{shojaie2022granger}.\nOur permutation-based approach circumvents the critical-slowing-down limitation by detecting temporal predictive improvement rather than variance or autocorrelation signatures.\nSpecification-curve analysis~\\citep{simonsohn2020specification} formalizes the sensitivity of conclusions to analytical choices; we adapt this framework to show that method choice alone shifts detection rates from 0\\% to 94\\%.\nThe multiverse analysis perspective~\\citep{steegen2016increasing,silberzahn2018many,coretta2023multidimensional,kapoor2024reforms} motivates our multi-method comparison: five methods on identical data produce conclusions ranging from universal significance to complete null, instantiating many-analysts divergence at the method level.\n",
```

```json
{

    "oldString": "We systematically map this \\emph{detectability landscape}---how detection outcomes vary across contamination levels ($\\alpha$), observation windows ($T$), and statistical methods---on Pythia-410M",
    "newString": "We systematically map this \\emph{detectability landscape}, examining how detection outcomes vary across contamination levels ($\\alpha$), observation windows ($T$), and statistical methods, on Pythia-410M",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen language models train iteratively on their own output, the training distribution progressively loses its tail modes, a phenomenon termed model collapse~\\citep{shumailov2024curse,alemohammad2024mad}.\nThis process can be triggered by vanishingly small synthetic fractions~\\citep{dohmatob2025strong}: lexical diversity narrows, calibration deteriorates, and each generation inherits the biases of its predecessors.\nAs machine-generated text grows prevalent on the web, such feedback loops pose a growing risk to production pipelines, and by the time standard quality metrics register degradation, the distribution may have already shifted irreversibly.\n\nThis threat motivates a fundamental question: \\emph{can we reliably detect the onset of model collapse?}\nThe answer is less clear than it might appear.\nIterative training produces short, noisy time series (often fewer than 15 points) where detection power drops sharply~\\citep{boettiger2012limits}, and model collapse is a first-order transition~\\citep{truong2026phase}, structurally ruling out the critical-slowing-down early-warning signals that ecologists rely on~\\citep{scheffer2009early} (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work}).\nMore fundamentally, no prior work has systematically compared detection methods on this problem; as we show, the choice of statistical test alone can shift conclusions from universal detection to complete null on identical data.\nDetection is effective only above $\\alpha_c \\approx 0.50$; substantial quality degradation (e.g., ${\\sim}40\\%$ distinct-1 decline) occurs undetected below this threshold, making the framework a confirmation tool for ongoing collapse rather than an early-warning system.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_phase_diagram.pdf}\n\\caption{Phase diagram (Pythia-410M, fp32, PermG). Cells show the fraction of FDR-significant canary$\\to$diversity pairs (out of 8) across $\\alpha$ and $T$; dashed curve: fitted boundary (Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c}); $\\diamond$: non-monotonic anomaly; $\\star$: dissolution at $T{=}25$.}\n\\label{fig:phase_diagram}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe systematically map this \\emph{detectability landscape}---how detection outcomes vary across contamination levels ($\\alpha$), observation windows ($T$), and statistical methods---on Pythia-410M~\\citep{biderman2023pythia} across 13 contamination levels with multi-seed replication (Figure~\\ref{fig:phase_diagram}).\nOur central finding is a steep detection onset: Permutation Granger causality (PermG) finds 0/8 significant canary$\\to$downstream pairs through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps to 6/8 (95\\% CI $[0.25, 0.92]$) within a 2-percentage-point interval at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:phase_diagram}), defining an empirical detection boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c}) that organizes three confirmed regimes (blind, onset, detection) plus one non-monotonic anomaly.\nThis transition emerges from a five-method comparison that itself reveals dramatic method sensitivity: null-control FPR spans 6.7\\% to 94.4\\% ($\\kappa{=}0.185$; Figure~\\ref{fig:method_comparison}), demonstrating that single-method analyses cannot reliably distinguish real signals from artifacts.\nCross-architecture evaluation at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ across five families (345M--6.9B) supports forward detection in 11/15 conditions, with four non-confirming cases isolating distinct failure modes (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\nOur canary claim is scoped to diversity metrics (distinct-$n$~\\citep{li2016diversity}); Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not causation (\\S\\ref{sec:method}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{We characterize a steep detection onset} (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}--\\ref{sec:dose_response}).\n    Detection jumps from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs (95\\% CI $[0.25, 0.92]$) within 2 percentage points of $\\alpha_c \\approx 0.50$, defining three confirmed regimes (blind, onset, detection) plus a non-monotonic anomaly at $\\alpha \\approx 0.60$.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{We map the detectability landscape} across contamination levels and observation windows (\\S\\ref{sec:t20}--\\ref{sec:theory}).\n    The detection boundary shifts leftward with longer observation, organizing the signal$\\times$resolution tradeoff that governs detection feasibility.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{A multi-method diagnostic toolkit supports reproducible analysis} (\\S\\ref{sec:mmct}).\n    Five methods yield null-control FPR from 6.7\\% to 94.4\\% ($\\kappa{=}0.185$); systematic comparison identifies PermG (6.7\\%, CI $[1.8\\%, 16.2\\%]$, 10~seeds) as the primary detector and exposes method sensitivity as a key obstacle.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{We demonstrate practical applications}: a cascade ordering where entropy precedes diversity by ${\\geq}1$ generation (Observations~\\ref{thm:entropy_leads}--\\ref{prop:div_lag}; 14/15 experiments) enables proof-of-concept predictive intervention reducing peak perplexity by 64\\% at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (\\S\\ref{sec:cascade}, \\S\\ref{sec:intervention}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 18,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 18,

          "+We systematically map this \\emph{detectability landscape}, examining how detection outcomes vary across contamination levels ($\\alpha$), observation windows ($T$), and statistical methods, on Pythia-410M~\\citep{biderman2023pythia} across 13 contamination levels with multi-seed replication (Figure~\\ref{fig:phase_diagram}).",
          " Our central finding is a steep detection onset: Permutation Granger causality (PermG) finds 0/8 significant canary$\\to$downstream pairs through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps to 6/8 (95\\% CI $[0.25, 0.92]$) within a 2-percentage-point interval at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:phase_diagram}), defining an empirical detection boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c}) that organizes three confirmed regimes (blind, onset, detection) plus one non-monotonic anomaly.",
```

```json
    "oldString": "\\FloatBarrier\n\\section{Related Work}",
    "newString": "\\section{Related Work}",
    "originalFile": "\\FloatBarrier\n\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\subsection{Model Collapse: Theory and Empirical Evidence}\n\\label{sec:rw_collapse}\n\nIterative training collapse was formalized by \\citet{shumailov2024curse} and \\citet{alemohammad2024mad}; \\citet{schaeffer2025position} systematize eight distinct notions of collapse; our operationalization aligns with their \\emph{distributional shift} family (definitions~D4--D6).\nTheoretical analyses sharpen these results: \\citet{dohmatob2024tale} recast collapse as changed scaling-law exponents, \\citet{dohmatob2025strong} proved collapse at vanishingly small synthetic fractions, and \\citet{truong2026phase} prove entropy collapse is a first-order transition, structurally ruling out critical-slowing-down early-warning signals~\\citep{scheffer2009early,scheffer2012anticipating}.\nEmpirically, \\citet{hataya2023will} demonstrate that generated images degrade downstream classification on ImageNet-scale data, and \\citet{kovac2025recursive} show that training data properties (lexical diversity, semantic diversity) modulate the magnitude of recursive collapse in LLMs.\nCritical mixture ratios separating qualitative outcomes have independent precedent: \\citet{kazdan2025collapse} map collapse conditions, \\citet{gu2024cmr} establish scaling laws for critical ratios, and \\citet{gu2025datamixing} show data mixing induces phase transitions in knowledge acquisition.\n\n\\subsection{Detection of Synthetic Data Contamination}\n\\label{sec:rw_detection}\n\nMonitoring metrics proposed for collapse include expected calibration error (ECE)~\\citep{du2025scales}, surplexity~\\citep{gambetta2024surplexity}, and machine-generated text detection~\\citep{drayson2025detection}; \\citet{shi2025closer} independently validate entropy as a collapse diagnostic in diffusion models; \\citet{buffa2026entropy} propose continuous entropy-based monitoring for LLM accuracy, though targeting task performance rather than distributional collapse.\nA complementary line of work pursues \\emph{proactive} contamination prevention via watermarking~\\citep{kirchenbauer2023watermark}; our distributional detection framework is \\emph{reactive}, identifying collapse from observed metric trajectories without requiring control over the generation process.\n\\citet{boettiger2012limits} establish fundamental power limits for early-warning detection in short time series ($T{<}25$), directly relevant to iterative training where observation windows are typically $T{=}5$--$20$.\nHowever, these works evaluate single metrics in isolation without cross-validating against alternative statistical tests.\nWe differ by applying five detection methods to identical data, mapping detectability across contamination regimes, and treating method sensitivity as a primary finding rather than a robustness check (detailed comparison: Appendix~\\ref{app:method_comparison}).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Methods for Distribution Shift}\n\\label{sec:rw_methods}\n\nFor temporal precedence testing, Granger causality~\\citep{granger1969investigating} is the standard framework, with neural extensions~\\citep{tank2021neural,runge2019detecting}; however, non-stationary series inflate rejection rates~\\citep{granger1974spurious}, and short series ($T{\\leq}25$) preclude reliable nonlinear estimation~\\citep{boettiger2012limits}.\nGranger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not true causation: bivariate tests cannot exclude confounders~\\citep{shojaie2022granger}.\nOur permutation-based approach circumvents the critical-slowing-down limitation by detecting temporal predictive improvement rather than variance or autocorrelation signatures.\nSpecification-curve analysis~\\citep{simonsohn2020specification} formalizes the sensitivity of conclusions to analytical choices; we adapt this framework to show that method choice alone shifts detection rates from 0\\% to 94\\%.\nThe multiverse analysis perspective~\\citep{steegen2016increasing,silberzahn2018many,coretta2023multidimensional,kapoor2024reforms} motivates our multi-method comparison: five methods on identical data produce conclusions ranging from universal significance to complete null, instantiating many-analysts divergence at the method level.\n",
```

```text
1	"""
2	Cross-architecture detection heatmap: PermG fwd detection across model families.
3	narrative_intent: "The canary mechanism generalizes across architectures,
4	with interpretable failure modes in specific conditions."
5	"""
6	import matplotlib
7	matplotlib.use('Agg')
8	import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
9	import numpy as np
10	import csv
11	
12	plt.rcParams.update({
13	    'font.family': 'DejaVu Sans',
14	    'font.size': 9,
15	    'axes.titlesize': 10,
16	    'axes.labelsize': 9,
17	    'xtick.labelsize': 8,
18	    'ytick.labelsize': 8,
19	    'legend.fontsize': 7,
20	    'figure.dpi': 300,
21	    'savefig.dpi': 300,
22	    'savefig.bbox': 'tight',
23	    'savefig.pad_inches': 0.03,
24	    'axes.spines.top': False,
25	    'axes.spines.right': False,
26	    'pdf.fonttype': 42,
27	    'ps.fonttype': 42,
28	})
29	
30	rows = []
31	with open('artifacts/cross_arch_table7.csv') as f:
32	    reader = csv.DictReader(f)
33	    for row in reader:
34	        if row['PermG_fwd'] and row['note'] not in ('RUNNING', 'RUNNING (~50h)', 'FAILED EXIT_CODE=1'):
35	            rows.append(row)
36	
37	def parse_frac(s):
38	    if not s:
39	        return np.nan
40	    num, den = s.split('/')
41	    return int(num) / int(den)
42	
43	models = []
44	permg_fwd = []
45	k3_vals = []
46	labels = []
47	
48	for r in rows:
49	    model = r['model']
50	    seed = r['seed']
51	    domain = r['domain']
52	    prec = r['precision']
53	    suffix = ''
54	    if domain == 'C4':
55	        suffix = ' (C4)'
56	    elif prec == 'bf16':
57	        suffix = ' (bf16)'
58	    label = f"{model} {seed}{suffix}"
59	    labels.append(label)
60	    permg_fwd.append(parse_frac(r['PermG_fwd']))
61	    k3_vals.append(parse_frac(r['K3_3sigma']))
62	
63	n = len(labels)
64	y = np.arange(n)
65	
66	fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(4.5, 3.2))
67	
68	bar_height = 0.35
69	bars1 = ax.barh(y + bar_height/2, [v*100 for v in permg_fwd], bar_height,
70	                color='#4A90C4', alpha=0.9, label='PermG fwd', edgecolor='#222222', linewidth=0.5)
71	bars2 = ax.barh(y - bar_height/2, [v*100 for v in k3_vals], bar_height,
72	                color='#B8D4E8', alpha=0.75, label=r'$K{\geq}3$ consensus')
73	
74	# Value labels
75	for i, (pv, kv) in enumerate(zip(permg_fwd, k3_vals)):
76	    ax.text(pv*100 + 1.5, i + bar_height/2, f'{int(pv*8)}/8',
77	            va='center', ha='left', fontsize=6, color='#4A90C4', fontweight='bold')
78	    ax.text(kv*100 + 1.5, i - bar_height/2, f'{int(kv*8)}/8',
79	            va='center', ha='left', fontsize=6, color='#7BBBD6')
80	
81	ax.set_yticks(y)
82	ax.set_yticklabels(labels, fontsize=7)
83	ax.set_xlabel('Detection rate (%)')
84	ax.set_xlim(0, 115)
85	ax.set_xticks([0, 25, 50, 75, 100])
86	ax.invert_yaxis()
87	
88	ax.xaxis.grid(True, alpha=0.15, linewidth=0.4)
89	ax.set_axisbelow(True)
90	
91	# 75% reference
92	ax.axvline(x=75, color='#4A90C4', linewidth=0.5, linestyle=':', alpha=0.4)
93	
94	leg = ax.legend(loc='lower right', frameon=True, framealpha=0.9,
95	                edgecolor='#cccccc', borderpad=0.3)
96	leg.get_frame().set_linewidth(0.4)
97	
98	plt.tight_layout()
99	plt.savefig('docs/paper/figures/fig_cross_arch_heatmap.pdf')
100	plt.savefig('docs/paper/figures/fig_cross_arch_heatmap.png')
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\"\"\"\nCross-architecture detection heatmap: PermG fwd detection across model families.\nnarrative_intent: \"The canary mechanism generalizes across architectures,\nwith interpretable failure modes in specific conditions.\"\n\"\"\"\nimport matplotlib\nmatplotlib.use('Agg')\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\nimport csv\n\nplt.rcParams.update({\n    'font.family': 'DejaVu Sans',\n    'font.size': 9,\n    'axes.titlesize': 10,\n    'axes.labelsize': 9,\n    'xtick.labelsize': 8,\n    'ytick.labelsize': 8,\n    'legend.fontsize': 7,\n    'figure.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.bbox': 'tight',\n    'savefig.pad_inches': 0.03,\n    'axes.spines.top': False,\n    'axes.spines.right': False,\n    'pdf.fonttype': 42,\n    'ps.fonttype': 42,\n})\n\nrows = []\nwith open('artifacts/cross_arch_table7.csv') as f:\n    reader = csv.DictReader(f)\n    for row in reader:\n        if row['PermG_fwd'] and row['note'] not in ('RUNNING', 'RUNNING (~50h)', 'FAILED EXIT_CODE=1'):\n            rows.append(row)\n\ndef parse_frac(s):\n    if not s:\n        return np.nan\n    num, den = s.split('/')\n    return int(num) / int(den)\n\nmodels = []\npermg_fwd = []\nk3_vals = []\nlabels = []\n\nfor r in rows:\n    model = r['model']\n    seed = r['seed']\n    domain = r['domain']\n    prec = r['precision']\n    suffix = ''\n    if domain == 'C4':\n        suffix = ' (C4)'\n    elif prec == 'bf16':\n        suffix = ' (bf16)'\n    label = f\"{model} {seed}{suffix}\"\n    labels.append(label)\n    permg_fwd.append(parse_frac(r['PermG_fwd']))\n    k3_vals.append(parse_frac(r['K3_3sigma']))\n\nn = len(labels)\ny = np.arange(n)\n\nfig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(4.5, 3.2))\n\nbar_height = 0.35\nbars1 = ax.barh(y + bar_height/2, [v*100 for v in permg_fwd], bar_height,\n                color='#4A90C4', alpha=0.9, label='PermG fwd', edgecolor='#222222', linewidth=0.5)\nbars2 = ax.barh(y - bar_height/2, [v*100 for v in k3_vals], bar_height,\n                color='#B8D4E8', alpha=0.75, label=r'$K{\\geq}3$ consensus')\n\n# Value labels\nfor i, (pv, kv) in enumerate(zip(permg_fwd, k3_vals)):\n    ax.text(pv*100 + 1.5, i + bar_height/2, f'{int(pv*8)}/8',\n            va='center', ha='left', fontsize=6, color='#4A90C4', fontweight='bold')\n    ax.text(kv*100 + 1.5, i - bar_height/2, f'{int(kv*8)}/8',\n            va='center', ha='left', fontsize=6, color='#7BBBD6')\n\nax.set_yticks(y)\nax.set_yticklabels(labels, fontsize=7)\nax.set_xlabel('Detection rate (%)')\nax.set_xlim(0, 115)\nax.set_xticks([0, 25, 50, 75, 100])\nax.invert_yaxis()\n\nax.xaxis.grid(True, alpha=0.15, linewidth=0.4)\nax.set_axisbelow(True)\n\n# 75% reference\nax.axvline(x=75, color='#4A90C4', linewidth=0.5, linestyle=':', alpha=0.4)\n\nleg = ax.legend(loc='lower right', frameon=True, framealpha=0.9,\n                edgecolor='#cccccc', borderpad=0.3)\nleg.get_frame().set_linewidth(0.4)\n\nplt.tight_layout()\nplt.savefig('docs/paper/figures/fig_cross_arch_heatmap.pdf')\nplt.savefig('docs/paper/figures/fig_cross_arch_heatmap.png')\nprint('Saved fig_cross_arch_heatmap.pdf + .png')\n",
```

```text
- Pythia-6.9B s42 (bf16): the "7/8" and "8/8" labels are behind the legend
- Qwen3.5-0.8B s42: the "8/8" labels might be partially hidden

So moving from lower-right to upper-right just shifted the problem. I need a position where the legend doesn't overlap with ANY bar labels.

Looking at the data:
- Short bars (with lots of space to the right): Pythia-1.4B s42 (2/8), SmolLM3-3B s42 (0/8, 3/8)


- Medium bars (~75%): most rows have 6/8
- Long bars (~100%): C4 rows and Qwen have 8/8

The bars cluster at ~25%, ~75%, or ~100%, so the right side is always crowded with labels. I should place the legend outside the plot area entirely, maybe below the x-axis label, or position it in the empty space where Pythia-1.4B s42's short bar leaves room around the top-third of the chart.

I'm calculating the axes coordinates—Pythia-1.4B s42 is at index 2, which puts it around y≈0.86 in normalized coordinates, and since the bar only extends to 25%, x=0.5 would be clear space. Let me try anchoring the legend at (0.6, 0.85) with center alignment, or better yet, switch to a horizontal legend positioned below the axes outside the plot area entirely.
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\"\"\"\nCross-architecture detection heatmap: PermG fwd detection across model families.\nnarrative_intent: \"The canary mechanism generalizes across architectures,\nwith interpretable failure modes in specific conditions.\"\n\"\"\"\nimport matplotlib\nmatplotlib.use('Agg')\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\nimport csv\n\nplt.rcParams.update({\n    'font.family': 'DejaVu Sans',\n    'font.size': 9,\n    'axes.titlesize': 10,\n    'axes.labelsize': 9,\n    'xtick.labelsize': 8,\n    'ytick.labelsize': 8,\n    'legend.fontsize': 7,\n    'figure.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.bbox': 'tight',\n    'savefig.pad_inches': 0.03,\n    'axes.spines.top': False,\n    'axes.spines.right': False,\n    'pdf.fonttype': 42,\n    'ps.fonttype': 42,\n})\n\nrows = []\nwith open('artifacts/cross_arch_table7.csv') as f:\n    reader = csv.DictReader(f)\n    for row in reader:\n        if row['PermG_fwd'] and row['note'] not in ('RUNNING', 'RUNNING (~50h)', 'FAILED EXIT_CODE=1'):\n            rows.append(row)\n\ndef parse_frac(s):\n    if not s:\n        return np.nan\n    num, den = s.split('/')\n    return int(num) / int(den)\n\nmodels = []\npermg_fwd = []\nk3_vals = []\nlabels = []\n\nfor r in rows:\n    model = r['model']\n    seed = r['seed']\n    domain = r['domain']\n    prec = r['precision']\n    suffix = ''\n    if domain == 'C4':\n        suffix = ' (C4)'\n    elif prec == 'bf16':\n        suffix = ' (bf16)'\n    label = f\"{model} {seed}{suffix}\"\n    labels.append(label)\n    permg_fwd.append(parse_frac(r['PermG_fwd']))\n    k3_vals.append(parse_frac(r['K3_3sigma']))\n\nn = len(labels)\ny = np.arange(n)\n\nfig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(4.5, 3.2))\n\nbar_height = 0.35\nbars1 = ax.barh(y + bar_height/2, [v*100 for v in permg_fwd], bar_height,\n                color='#4A90C4', alpha=0.9, label='PermG fwd', edgecolor='#222222', linewidth=0.5)\nbars2 = ax.barh(y - bar_height/2, [v*100 for v in k3_vals], bar_height,\n                color='#B8D4E8', alpha=0.75, label=r'$K{\\geq}3$ consensus')\n\n# Value labels\nfor i, (pv, kv) in enumerate(zip(permg_fwd, k3_vals)):\n    ax.text(pv*100 + 1.5, i + bar_height/2, f'{int(pv*8)}/8',\n            va='center', ha='left', fontsize=6, color='#4A90C4', fontweight='bold')\n    ax.text(kv*100 + 1.5, i - bar_height/2, f'{int(kv*8)}/8',\n            va='center', ha='left', fontsize=6, color='#7BBBD6')\n\nax.set_yticks(y)\nax.set_yticklabels(labels, fontsize=7)\nax.set_xlabel('Detection rate (%)')\nax.set_xlim(0, 115)\nax.set_xticks([0, 25, 50, 75, 100])\nax.invert_yaxis()\n\nax.xaxis.grid(True, alpha=0.15, linewidth=0.4)\nax.set_axisbelow(True)\n\n# 75% reference\nax.axvline(x=75, color='#4A90C4', linewidth=0.5, linestyle=':', alpha=0.4)\n\nleg = ax.legend(loc='upper right', frameon=True, framealpha=0.9,\n                edgecolor='#cccccc', borderpad=0.3)\nleg.get_frame().set_linewidth(0.4)\n\nplt.tight_layout()\nplt.savefig('docs/paper/figures/fig_cross_arch_heatmap.pdf')\nplt.savefig('docs/paper/figures/fig_cross_arch_heatmap.png')\nprint('Saved fig_cross_arch_heatmap.pdf + .png')\n",
```

```json
    "newString": "leg = ax.legend(loc='upper center', bbox_to_anchor=(0.5, -0.13),\n                ncol=2, frameon=True, framealpha=0.9,\n                edgecolor='#cccccc', borderpad=0.3)\nleg.get_frame().set_linewidth(0.4)\n\nfig.subplots_adjust(bottom=0.18)",
    "originalFile": "\"\"\"\nCross-architecture detection heatmap: PermG fwd detection across model families.\nnarrative_intent: \"The canary mechanism generalizes across architectures,\nwith interpretable failure modes in specific conditions.\"\n\"\"\"\nimport matplotlib\nmatplotlib.use('Agg')\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\nimport csv\n\nplt.rcParams.update({\n    'font.family': 'DejaVu Sans',\n    'font.size': 9,\n    'axes.titlesize': 10,\n    'axes.labelsize': 9,\n    'xtick.labelsize': 8,\n    'ytick.labelsize': 8,\n    'legend.fontsize': 7,\n    'figure.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.bbox': 'tight',\n    'savefig.pad_inches': 0.03,\n    'axes.spines.top': False,\n    'axes.spines.right': False,\n    'pdf.fonttype': 42,\n    'ps.fonttype': 42,\n})\n\nrows = []\nwith open('artifacts/cross_arch_table7.csv') as f:\n    reader = csv.DictReader(f)\n    for row in reader:\n        if row['PermG_fwd'] and row['note'] not in ('RUNNING', 'RUNNING (~50h)', 'FAILED EXIT_CODE=1'):\n            rows.append(row)\n\ndef parse_frac(s):\n    if not s:\n        return np.nan\n    num, den = s.split('/')\n    return int(num) / int(den)\n\nmodels = []\npermg_fwd = []\nk3_vals = []\nlabels = []\n\nfor r in rows:\n    model = r['model']\n    seed = r['seed']\n    domain = r['domain']\n    prec = r['precision']\n    suffix = ''\n    if domain == 'C4':\n        suffix = ' (C4)'\n    elif prec == 'bf16':\n        suffix = ' (bf16)'\n    label = f\"{model} {seed}{suffix}\"\n    labels.append(label)\n    permg_fwd.append(parse_frac(r['PermG_fwd']))\n    k3_vals.append(parse_frac(r['K3_3sigma']))\n\nn = len(labels)\ny = np.arange(n)\n\nfig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(4.5, 3.2))\n\nbar_height = 0.35\nbars1 = ax.barh(y + bar_height/2, [v*100 for v in permg_fwd], bar_height,\n                color='#4A90C4', alpha=0.9, label='PermG fwd', edgecolor='#222222', linewidth=0.5)\nbars2 = ax.barh(y - bar_height/2, [v*100 for v in k3_vals], bar_height,\n                color='#B8D4E8', alpha=0.75, label=r'$K{\\geq}3$ consensus')\n\n# Value labels\nfor i, (pv, kv) in enumerate(zip(permg_fwd, k3_vals)):\n    ax.text(pv*100 + 1.5, i + bar_height/2, f'{int(pv*8)}/8',\n            va='center', ha='left', fontsize=6, color='#4A90C4', fontweight='bold')\n    ax.text(kv*100 + 1.5, i - bar_height/2, f'{int(kv*8)}/8',\n            va='center', ha='left', fontsize=6, color='#7BBBD6')\n\nax.set_yticks(y)\nax.set_yticklabels(labels, fontsize=7)\nax.set_xlabel('Detection rate (%)')\nax.set_xlim(0, 115)\nax.set_xticks([0, 25, 50, 75, 100])\nax.invert_yaxis()\n\nax.xaxis.grid(True, alpha=0.15, linewidth=0.4)\nax.set_axisbelow(True)\n\n# 75% reference\nax.axvline(x=75, color='#4A90C4', linewidth=0.5, linestyle=':', alpha=0.4)\n\nleg = ax.legend(loc='upper center', bbox_to_anchor=(0.5, -0.10),\n                ncol=2, frameon=True, framealpha=0.9,\n                edgecolor='#cccccc', borderpad=0.3)\nleg.get_frame().set_linewidth(0.4)\n\nplt.tight_layout()\nplt.savefig('docs/paper/figures/fig_cross_arch_heatmap.pdf')\nplt.savefig('docs/paper/figures/fig_cross_arch_heatmap.png')\nprint('Saved fig_cross_arch_heatmap.pdf + .png')\n",
```

```json
    "oldString": "leg = ax.legend(loc='upper center', bbox_to_anchor=(0.5, -0.13),\n                ncol=2, frameon=True, framealpha=0.9,\n                edgecolor='#cccccc', borderpad=0.3)\nleg.get_frame().set_linewidth(0.4)\n\nfig.subplots_adjust(bottom=0.18)",
    "newString": "leg = ax.legend(loc='lower center', bbox_to_anchor=(0.5, 1.02),\n                ncol=2, frameon=True, framealpha=0.9,\n                edgecolor='#cccccc', borderpad=0.3)\nleg.get_frame().set_linewidth(0.4)\n\nfig.subplots_adjust(top=0.92)",
    "originalFile": "\"\"\"\nCross-architecture detection heatmap: PermG fwd detection across model families.\nnarrative_intent: \"The canary mechanism generalizes across architectures,\nwith interpretable failure modes in specific conditions.\"\n\"\"\"\nimport matplotlib\nmatplotlib.use('Agg')\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\nimport csv\n\nplt.rcParams.update({\n    'font.family': 'DejaVu Sans',\n    'font.size': 9,\n    'axes.titlesize': 10,\n    'axes.labelsize': 9,\n    'xtick.labelsize': 8,\n    'ytick.labelsize': 8,\n    'legend.fontsize': 7,\n    'figure.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.bbox': 'tight',\n    'savefig.pad_inches': 0.03,\n    'axes.spines.top': False,\n    'axes.spines.right': False,\n    'pdf.fonttype': 42,\n    'ps.fonttype': 42,\n})\n\nrows = []\nwith open('artifacts/cross_arch_table7.csv') as f:\n    reader = csv.DictReader(f)\n    for row in reader:\n        if row['PermG_fwd'] and row['note'] not in ('RUNNING', 'RUNNING (~50h)', 'FAILED EXIT_CODE=1'):\n            rows.append(row)\n\ndef parse_frac(s):\n    if not s:\n        return np.nan\n    num, den = s.split('/')\n    return int(num) / int(den)\n\nmodels = []\npermg_fwd = []\nk3_vals = []\nlabels = []\n\nfor r in rows:\n    model = r['model']\n    seed = r['seed']\n    domain = r['domain']\n    prec = r['precision']\n    suffix = ''\n    if domain == 'C4':\n        suffix = ' (C4)'\n    elif prec == 'bf16':\n        suffix = ' (bf16)'\n    label = f\"{model} {seed}{suffix}\"\n    labels.append(label)\n    permg_fwd.append(parse_frac(r['PermG_fwd']))\n    k3_vals.append(parse_frac(r['K3_3sigma']))\n\nn = len(labels)\ny = np.arange(n)\n\nfig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(4.5, 3.2))\n\nbar_height = 0.35\nbars1 = ax.barh(y + bar_height/2, [v*100 for v in permg_fwd], bar_height,\n                color='#4A90C4', alpha=0.9, label='PermG fwd', edgecolor='#222222', linewidth=0.5)\nbars2 = ax.barh(y - bar_height/2, [v*100 for v in k3_vals], bar_height,\n                color='#B8D4E8', alpha=0.75, label=r'$K{\\geq}3$ consensus')\n\n# Value labels\nfor i, (pv, kv) in enumerate(zip(permg_fwd, k3_vals)):\n    ax.text(pv*100 + 1.5, i + bar_height/2, f'{int(pv*8)}/8',\n            va='center', ha='left', fontsize=6, color='#4A90C4', fontweight='bold')\n    ax.text(kv*100 + 1.5, i - bar_height/2, f'{int(kv*8)}/8',\n            va='center', ha='left', fontsize=6, color='#7BBBD6')\n\nax.set_yticks(y)\nax.set_yticklabels(labels, fontsize=7)\nax.set_xlabel('Detection rate (%)')\nax.set_xlim(0, 115)\nax.set_xticks([0, 25, 50, 75, 100])\nax.invert_yaxis()\n\nax.xaxis.grid(True, alpha=0.15, linewidth=0.4)\nax.set_axisbelow(True)\n\n# 75% reference\nax.axvline(x=75, color='#4A90C4', linewidth=0.5, linestyle=':', alpha=0.4)\n\nleg = ax.legend(loc='upper center', bbox_to_anchor=(0.5, -0.13),\n                ncol=2, frameon=True, framealpha=0.9,\n                edgecolor='#cccccc', borderpad=0.3)\nleg.get_frame().set_linewidth(0.4)\n\nfig.subplots_adjust(bottom=0.18)\nplt.savefig('docs/paper/figures/fig_cross_arch_heatmap.pdf')\nplt.savefig('docs/paper/figures/fig_cross_arch_heatmap.png')\nprint('Saved fig_cross_arch_heatmap.pdf + .png')\n",
```

```json
  "sessionId": "36cb22b8-86b0-4028-b0c2-9fe60f48bf51",
  "content": "[飞书用户 用户]: 📐 论文自检任务：与 ACL 官方模板对齐 + 控制正文页数\n\n请打开下面这个 ACL 官方模板，逐行、非常仔细地和我们论文目前使用的 template 对比，找出任何差异并对齐。\n\n官方模板：\nhttps://www.overleaf.com/latex/templates/association-for-computational-linguistics-acl-conference/jvxskxpnznfj\n\n---\n\n任务要求：\n\n1. 检测任何差异\n   把官方模板和我们当前论文用的 template 做完整 diff，任何差异都不能漏掉，包括但不限于：\n   - 文档类参数：\\documentclass[...]{acl} 的所有选项是否一致\n   - preamble 中的 \\usepackage：包名、版本、参数、顺序\n   - 字体、字号、行距、页边距设置\n   - 标题、作者、机构、邮箱区域的格式\n   - Abstract 区域的环境定义\n   - section / subsection / paragraph 的样式\n   - 引用、参考文献的样式（natbib? bibstyle?）\n   - 脚注样式\n   - 表格、图片的默认样式\n   - 公式编号、定理环境\n   - 页眉页脚、页码\n   - hyperref / url 的设置\n   - 任何被我们注释掉、替换、增加的行\n\n2. 全部向官方文档对齐\n   发现任何与官方模板不一致的地方，全部改成官方模板的写法。除非有非常明确的必要原因（如 venue 要求的差异），否则不要保留任何自定义改动。如果某处差异确实有理由保留，请在汇报里单独说明原因。\n\n3. 控制正文页数在 8 页左右\n   - 正文（main text，不含 references / appendix）必须在 8 页左右\n   - 严格不超过 8 页（超 1 行都不行）\n   - 也不能比 8 页少太多——如果当前只有 5、6 页，说明内容不够充实，需要补充：\n     * 增加实验分析 / ablation / 定性例子\n     * 把过于简略的方法描述展开\n     * 增加 motivation、related work、discussion 等\n   - 目标是恰好填满 8 页正文\n\n---\n\n完成后请汇报：\n\n(a) 我们的 template 和官方模板有哪些差异？每一处都列出来\n(b) 这些差异分别是怎么修复对齐的？还是有保留的（保留的请说明原因）\n(c) 修改后正文目前是几页？如果不到 8 页，准备补充哪些内容？如果超过 8 页，准备压缩哪些内容？"
}
```

[飞书用户 用户]: 📐 论文自检任务：与 ACL 官方模板对齐 + 控制正文页数

请打开下面这个 ACL 官方模板，逐行、非常仔细地和我们论文目前使用的 template 对比，找出任何差异并对齐。

官方模板：
https://www.overleaf.com/latex/templates/association-for-computational-linguistics-acl-conference/jvxskxpnznfj

---

任务要求：

1. 检测任何差异
   把官方模板和我们当前论文用的 template 做完整 diff，任何差异都不能漏掉，包括但不限于：
   - 文档类参数：\documentclass[...]{acl} 的所有选项是否一致
   - preamble 中的 \usepackage：包名、版本、参数、顺序
   - 字体、字号、行距、页边距设置
   - 标题、作者、机构、邮箱区域的格式
   - Abstract 区域的环境定义
   - section / subsection / paragraph 的样式
   - 引用、参考文献的样式（natbib? bibstyle?）
   - 脚注样式
   - 表格、图片的默认样式
   - 公式编号、定理环境
   - 页眉页脚、页码
   - hyperref / url 的设置
   - 任何被我们注释掉、替换、增加的行

2. 全部向官方文档对齐
   发现任何与官方模板不一致的地方，全部改成官方模板的写法。除非有非常明确的必要原因（如 venue 要求的差异），否则不要保留任何自定义改动。如果某处差异确实有理由保留，请在汇报里单独说明原因。

3. 控制正文页数在 8 页左右
   - 正文（main text，不含 references / appendix）必须在 8 页左右
   - 严格不超过 8 页（超 1 行都不行）
   - 也不能比 8 页少太多——如果当前只有 5、6 页，说明内容不够充实，需要补充：
     * 增加实验分析 / ablation / 定性例子
     * 把过于简略的方法描述展开
     * 增加 motivation、related work、discussion 等
   - 目标是恰好填满 8 页正文

---

完成后请汇报：

(a) 我们的 template 和官方模板有哪些差异？每一处都列出来
(b) 这些差异分别是怎么修复对齐的？还是有保留的（保留的请说明原因）
(c) 修改后正文目前是几页？如果不到 8 页，准备补充哪些内容？如果超过 8 页，准备压缩哪些内容？

```text
# ACL Conference Template - Download Information

## Direct Download Link
The page provides an **"Open as Template"** button that launches the template in Overleaf's editor, but does not offer a direct ZIP file download. To access source files, you would need to:

1. Click "Open as Template" to work in Overleaf
2. Access the GitHub repository linked in the author field: `https://github.com/acl-org/acl-style-files/`

## Template Contents
According to the documentation, the package includes:
- `acl_latex.tex` (LaTeX source)
- `acl.sty` (style file)
- `acl_natbib.bst` (bibliography style)
- `custom.bib` (example bibliography)
- `anthology.bib` (ACL Anthology bibliography)

## Document Class Configuration
The template uses: `\documentclass[11pt]{article}`

The style file loads via `\usepackage[review]{acl}` for review versions, or `\usepackage{acl}` for final versions.

## Metadata
- **License:** Creative Commons CC BY 4.0
- **Last Updated:** 10 months ago
- **Tag:** Conference Paper
```

```text
107	\begin{document}
108	\maketitle
109	\begin{abstract}
110	This document is a supplement to the general instructions for *ACL authors. It contains instructions for using the \LaTeX{} style files for ACL conferences.
111	The document itself conforms to its own specifications, and is therefore an example of what your manuscript should look like.
112	These instructions should be used both for papers submitted for review and for final versions of accepted papers.
113	\end{abstract}
114	
115	\section{Introduction}
116	
117	These instructions are for authors submitting papers to *ACL conferences using \LaTeX. They are not self-contained. All authors must follow the general instructions for *ACL proceedings,\footnote{\url{http://acl-org.github.io/ACLPUB/formatting.html}} and this document contains additional instructions for the \LaTeX{} style files.
118	
119	The templates include the \LaTeX{} source of this document (\texttt{acl\_latex.tex}),
120	the \LaTeX{} style file used to format it (\texttt{acl.sty}),
121	an ACL bibliography style (\texttt{acl\_natbib.bst}),
122	an example bibliography (\texttt{custom.bib}),
123	and the bibliography for the ACL Anthology (\texttt{anthology.bib}).
124	
125	\section{Engines}
126	
127	To produce a PDF file, pdf\LaTeX{} is strongly recommended (over original \LaTeX{} plus dvips+ps2pdf or dvipdf).
128	The style file \texttt{acl.sty} can also be used with
129	lua\LaTeX{} and
130	Xe\LaTeX{}, which are especially suitable for text in non-Latin scripts.
131	The file \texttt{acl\_lualatex.tex} in this repository provides
132	an example of how to use \texttt{acl.sty} with either
133	lua\LaTeX{} or
134	Xe\LaTeX{}.
135	
136	\section{Preamble}
137	
138	The first line of the file must be
139	\begin{quote}
140	\begin{verbatim}
141	\documentclass[11pt]{article}
142	\end{verbatim}
143	\end{quote}
144	
145	To load the style file in the review version:
146	\begin{quote}
147	\begin{verbatim}
148	\usepackage[review]{acl}
149	\end{verbatim}
150	\end{quote}
151	For the final version, omit the \verb|review| option:
152	\begin{quote}
153	\begin{verbatim}
154	\usepackage{acl}
155	\end{verbatim}
156	\end{quote}
157	
158	To use Times Roman, put the following in the preamble:
159	\begin{quote}
160	\begin{verbatim}
161	\usepackage{times}
162	\end{verbatim}
163	\end{quote}
164	(Alternatives like txfonts or newtx are also acceptable.)
165	
166	Please see the \LaTeX{} source of this document for comments on other packages that may be useful.
167	
168	Set the title and author using \verb|\title| and \verb|\author|. Within the author list, format multiple authors using \verb|\and| and \verb|\And| and \verb|\AND|; please see the \LaTeX{} source for examples.
169	
170	By default, the box containing the title and author names is set to the minimum of 5 cm. If you need more space, include the following in the preamble:
171	\begin{quote}
172	\begin{verbatim}
173	\setlength\titlebox{&lt;dim>}
174	\end{verbatim}
175	\end{quote}
176	where \verb|&lt;dim>| is replaced with a length. Do not set this length smaller than 5 cm.
177	
178	\section{Document Body}
179	
180	\subsection{Footnotes}
181	
182	Footnotes are inserted with the \verb|\footnote| command.\footnote{This is a footnote.}
183	
184	\subsection{Tables and figures}
185	
186	See Table~\ref{tab:accents} for an example of a table and its caption.
187	\textbf{Do not override the default caption sizes.}
188	
189	\begin{table}
190	  \centering
191	  \begin{tabular}{lc}
192	    \hline
193	    \textbf{Command} & \textbf{Output} \\
194	    \hline
195	    \verb|{\"a}|     & {\"a}           \\
196	    \verb|{\^e}|     & {\^e}           \\
197	    \verb|{\`i}|     & {\`i}           \\
198	    \verb|{\.I}|     & {\.I}           \\
199	    \verb|{\o}|      & {\o}            \\
200	    \verb|{\'u}|     & {\'u}           \\
201	    \verb|{\aa}|     & {\aa}           \\\hline
202	  \end{tabular}
203	  \begin{tabular}{lc}
204	    \hline
205	    \textbf{Command} & \textbf{Output} \\
206	    \hline
207	    \verb|{\c c}|    & {\c c}          \\
208	    \verb|{\u g}|    & {\u g}          \\
209	    \verb|{\l}|      & {\l}            \\

212	    \verb|{\v r}|    & {\v r}          \\
213	    \verb|{\ss}|     & {\ss}           \\
214	    \hline
215	  \end{tabular}
216	  \caption{Example commands for accented characters, to be used in, \emph{e.g.}, Bib\TeX{} entries.}
217	  \label{tab:accents}
218	\end{table}
219	
220	As much as possible, fonts in figures should conform
221	to the document fonts. See Figure~\ref{fig:experiments} for an example of a figure and its caption.
222	
223	Using the \verb|graphicx| package graphics files can be included within figure
224	environment at an appropriate point within the text.
225	The \verb|graphicx| package supports various optional arguments to control the
226	appearance of the figure.
227	You must include it explicitly in the \LaTeX{} preamble (after the
228	\verb|\documentclass| declaration and before \verb|\begin{document}|) using
229	\verb|\usepackage{graphicx}|.

245	
246	\subsection{Hyperlinks}
247	
248	Users of older versions of \LaTeX{} may encounter the following error during compilation:
249	\begin{quote}
250	\verb|\pdfendlink| ended up in different nesting level than \verb|\pdfstartlink|.
251	\end{quote}
252	This happens when pdf\LaTeX{} is used and a citation splits across a page boundary. The best way to fix this is to upgrade \LaTeX{} to 2018-12-01 or later.
253	
254	\subsection{Citations}
255	
256	\begin{table*}
257	  \centering
258	  \begin{tabular}{lll}
259	    \hline
260	    \textbf{Output}           & \textbf{natbib command} & \textbf{ACL only command} \\
261	    \hline
262	    \citep{Gusfield:97}       & \verb|\citep|           &                           \\
263	    \citealp{Gusfield:97}     & \verb|\citealp|         &                           \\
264	    \citet{Gusfield:97}       & \verb|\citet|           &                           \\
265	    \citeyearpar{Gusfield:97} & \verb|\citeyearpar|     &                           \\
266	    \citeposs{Gusfield:97}    &                         & \verb|\citeposs|          \\
267	    \hline
268	  \end{tabular}
269	  \caption{\label{citation-guide}
270	    Citation commands supported by the style file.
271	    The style is based on the natbib package and supports all natbib citation commands.
272	    It also supports commands defined in previous ACL style files for compatibility.
273	  }
274	\end{table*}
275	
276	Table~\ref{citation-guide} shows the syntax supported by the style files.
277	We encourage you to use the natbib styles.
278	You can use the command \verb|\citet| (cite in text) to get ``author (year)'' citations, like this citation to a paper by \citet{Gusfield:97}.
279	You can use the command \verb|\citep| (cite in parentheses) to get ``(author, year)'' citations \citep{Gusfield:97}.
280	You can use the command \verb|\citealp| (alternative cite without parentheses) to get ``author, year'' citations, which is useful for using citations within parentheses (e.g. \citealp{Gusfield:97}).
281	
282	A possessive citation can be made with the command \verb|\citeposs|.
283	This is not a standard natbib command, so it is generally not compatible
284	with other style files.
285	
286	\subsection{References}
287	
288	\nocite{Ando2005,andrew2007scalable,rasooli-tetrault-2015}
289	
290	The \LaTeX{} and Bib\TeX{} style files provided roughly follow the American Psychological Association format.
291	If your own bib file is named \texttt{custom.bib}, then placing the following before any appendices in your \LaTeX{} file will generate the references section for you:
292	\begin{quote}
293	\begin{verbatim}
294	\bibliography{custom}
295	\end{verbatim}
296	\end{quote}
297	
298	You can obtain the complete ACL Anthology as a Bib\TeX{} file from \url{https://aclweb.org/anthology/anthology.bib.gz}.
299	To include both the Anthology and your own .bib file, use the following instead of the above.
300	\begin{quote}
301	\begin{verbatim}
302	\bibliography{anthology,custom}
303	\end{verbatim}
304	\end{quote}
305	
306	Please see Section~\ref{sec:bibtex} for information on preparing Bib\TeX{} files.
307	
308	\subsection{Equations}
309	
310	An example equation is shown below:
311	\begin{equation}
312	  \label{eq:example}
313	  A = \pi r^2
314	\end{equation}
315	
316	Labels for equation numbers, sections, subsections, figures and tables
317	are all defined with the \verb|\label{label}| command and cross references
318	to them are made with the \verb|\ref{label}| command.
319	
320	This an example cross-reference to Equation~\ref{eq:example}.
321	
322	\subsection{Appendices}
323	
324	Use \verb|\appendix| before any appendix section to switch the section numbering over to letters. See Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix} for an example.
325	
326	\section{Bib\TeX{} Files}
327	\label{sec:bibtex}
328	
329	Unicode cannot be used in Bib\TeX{} entries, and some ways of typing special characters can disrupt Bib\TeX's alphabetization. The recommended way of typing special characters is shown in Table~\ref{tab:accents}.
330	
331	Please ensure that Bib\TeX{} records contain DOIs or URLs when possible, and for all the ACL materials that you reference.
332	Use the \verb|doi| field for DOIs and the \verb|url| field for URLs.
333	If a Bib\TeX{} entry has a URL or DOI field, the paper title in the references section will appear as a hyperlink to the paper, using the hyperref \LaTeX{} package.
334	
335	\section*{Limitations}
336	
337	This document does not cover the content requirements for ACL or any
338	other specific venue.  Check the author instructions for
339	information on
340	maximum page lengths, the required ``Limitations'' section,
341	and so on.
```

````text
Now I have both the official template and our paper. Let me do a detailed comparison.

```latex
\documentclass[11pt]{article}

\usepackage{times}
\usepackage{latexsym}
\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}
\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}
\usepackage{microtype}
\usepackage{inconsolata}  % <-- THIS IS IN OFFICIAL BUT NOT IN OURS


\usepackage{graphicx}
```

**Our paper (`main.tex`):**
```latex
\documentclass[11pt]{article}
\usepackage[review]{acl}
\setlength{\linenumbersep}{12pt}  % <-- CUSTOM, overrides acl.sty default of 1.6cm

\usepackage{times}
\usepackage{latexsym}
\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}
\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}
\usepackage{microtype}

% MISSING: \usepackage{inconsolata}

% EXTRA
```

Comparing the preambles, I notice we're missing the inconsolata font package and graphicx, while we've added a custom line number spacing adjustment that overrides the ACL style defaults.

Now looking at what's in our actual paper beyond the official template, we're importing math packages like amsmath and amssymb, plus utilities for tables, figures, and algorithms. We've also defined custom theorem environments for our proofs and propositions.

Comparing against the official ACL style, I notice we're overriding the line number spacing to 12pt instead of the default 1.6cm—that's a much tighter gap that was probably added to fix overlap issues during review, but since we want strict alignment with the official template, I should remove it. We're also missing the inconsolata package for better typewriter fonts, so I need to add that back in.

For the extra packages like amsmath, amssymb, booktabs, subcaption, and the algorithm/theorem environments—these are all legitimate additions for our paper's actual content needs, and the official template explicitly notes that authors should add packages as required, so keeping them makes sense.

The `\raggedbottom` command is overriding the official `\flushbottom` setting from acl.sty, which was added to fix line spacing issues. Since the user wants full alignment with the official template, I should remove this unless there's a compelling reason to keep it—let me test whether removing it causes visible problems.

The bibliography filename difference is inconsequential, and using `\author{Anonymous}` works fine since acl.sty handles anonymization automatically in review mode. I notice the official template includes an `\appendix` command before the appendix section, which we're missing.

Now I need to verify whether our acl.sty file matches the official version, since that's the real determinant of whether our styling is correct.
````

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The user (陈卓凡) and collaborators (曹晓玉, 周俊伟) are formatting an EMNLP 2026 paper with an 8-page main text limit. The ongoing session involved:
   - Converting main text figures/tables from `[H]` to `[t]` float placement (曹晓玉)
   - Converting Figure 3 and Figure 6 from vertical 2×1 to horizontal 1×2 side-by-side subfigure layout (曹晓玉)
   - Fixing Figure 5 legend occlusion of bar labels (周俊伟), then moving legend to top (曹晓玉)
   - Fixing all issues from a paper audit report (2 critical, 187 warnings)
   - Comparing paper template with official ACL template from GitHub and aligning differences (用户/陈卓凡)
   - PDF must be uploaded to Feishu after every round of changes

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - `figure*[t]` in two-column LaTeX appears at top of the NEXT page after declaration — must declare on page N-1 to appear on page N
   - `\flushbottom` (official ACL default) vs `\raggedbottom` — flushbottom stretches inter-paragraph spacing to equalize column heights
   - `\usepackage{inconsolata}` is optional in ACL template ("may be commented out")
   - `\linenumbersep` default in acl.sty is 1.6cm; we had overridden to 12pt
   - γ symbol conflict: same symbol used for cascade margin and power-law exponent — resolved by renaming power-law exponent to η
   - Em-dash (`---`) overuse is an AI writing marker — replaced with commas, parentheses, semicolons
   - matplotlib `plt.subplots(2, 1)` → `plt.subplots(1, 2)` for side-by-side panels
   - `bbox_to_anchor` for precise legend positioning outside axes area

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/main.tex`**
     - Central document file. Template alignment changes made here.
     - Removed `\setlength{\linenumbersep}{12pt}` (was after `\usepackage[review]{acl}`)
     - Removed `\raggedbottom` (was after `\begin{document}`)
     - Attempted to add `\usepackage{inconsolata}` but package not installed; removed since official says optional
     - Current state:

     - Key current state for detection boundary:

4. Errors and Fixes:
   - **`figure*[t]` placement**: Figure 3 kept landing on page 6 instead of 5. Root cause: `figure*[t]` appears on the page AFTER where it's declared. Fix: moved declaration progressively earlier until it was after Algorithm 1 (page 4 content), causing it to appear on page 5.
   - **Figure 4 not on page 6**: After Figure 3 fix, Figure 4 landed on page 7. Fix: moved Figure 4 declaration earlier to start of section 4.3.
   - **Figure 5 legend occlusion**: Legend at lower-right blocked bottom bars' numbers. Three iterations: upper-right (blocked top bars) → bottom outside (overlapped x-axis label) → bottom with proper spacing → finally moved to top per user request.
   - **`\usepackage{inconsolata}` compilation failure**: Package not installed on system. Fix: removed it (official template marks it as optional).
   - **Page count went from 37→38**: Converting Figure 3 to double-column added vertical space. Acceptable since main text stayed at 8 pages.

5. Problem Solving:
   - Float placement in two-column LaTeX required iterative positioning of `figure*[t]` declarations
   - Symbol conflict resolution (γ→η) required careful grep across all files to find every instance, distinguishing between cascade margin γ (kept) and power-law exponent γ (changed to η)
   - Em-dash removal required contextual replacement (not mechanical find-replace) — each instance replaced with the most natural alternative (comma, parentheses, semicolon, or sentence restructuring)
   - ACL template alignment required downloading official files from GitHub and doing systematic comparison

It contains instructions for using the \\LaTeX{} style files for ACL conferences.\nThe document itself conforms to its own specifications, and is therefore an example of what your manuscript should look like.\nThese instructions should be used both for papers submitted for review and for final versions of accepted papers.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nThese instructions are for authors submitting papers to *ACL conferences using \\LaTeX. They are not self-contained. All authors must follow the general instructions for *ACL proceedings,\\footnote{\\url{http://acl-org.github.io/ACLPUB/formatting.html}} and this document contains additional instructions for the \\LaTeX{} style files.\n\nThe templates include the \\LaTeX{} source of this document (\\texttt{acl\\_latex.tex}),\nthe \\LaTeX{} style file used to format it (\\texttt{acl.sty}),\nan ACL bibliography style (\\texttt{acl\\_natbib.bst}),\nan example bibliography (\\texttt{custom.bib}),\nand the bibliography for the ACL Anthology (\\texttt{anthology.bib}).\n\n\\section{Engines}\n\nTo produce a PDF file, pdf\\LaTeX{} is strongly recommended (over original \\LaTeX{} plus dvips+ps2pdf or dvipdf).\nThe style file \\texttt{acl.sty} can also be used with\nlua\\LaTeX{} and\nXe\\LaTeX{}, which are especially suitable for text in non-Latin scripts.\nThe file \\texttt{acl\\_lualatex.tex} in this repository provides\nan example of how to use \\texttt{acl.sty} with either\nlua\\LaTeX{} or\nXe\\LaTeX{}.\n\n\\section{Preamble}\n\nThe first line of the file must be\n\\begin{quote}\n\\begin{verbatim}\n\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\\end{verbatim}\n\\end{quote}\n\nTo load the style file in the review version:\n\\begin{quote}\n\\begin{verbatim}\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\\end{verbatim}\n\\end{quote}\nFor the final version, omit the \\verb|review| option:\n\\begin{quote}\n\\begin{verbatim}\n\\usepackage{acl}\n\\end{verbatim}\n\\end{quote}\n\nTo use Times Roman, put the following in the preamble:\n\\begin{quote}\n\\begin{verbatim}\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\end{verbatim}\n\\end{quote}\n(Alternatives like txfonts or newtx are also acceptable.)\n\nPlease see the \\LaTeX{} source of this document for comments on other packages that may be useful.\n\nSet the title and author using \\verb|\\title| and \\verb|\\author|. Within the author list, format multiple authors using \\verb|\\and| and \\verb|\\And| and \\verb|\\AND|; please see the \\LaTeX{} source for examples.\n\nBy default, the box containing the title and author names is set to the minimum of 5 cm. If you need more space, include the following in the preamble:\n\\begin{quote}\n\\begin{verbatim}\n\\setlength\\titlebox{&lt;dim>}\n\\end{verbatim}\n\\end{quote}\nwhere \\verb|&lt;dim>| is replaced with a length. Do not set this length smaller than 5 cm.\n\n\\section{Document Body}\n\n\\subsection{Footnotes}\n\nFootnotes are inserted with the \\verb|\\footnote| command.\\footnote{This is a footnote.}\n\n\\subsection{Tables and figures}\n\nSee Table~\\ref{tab:accents} for an example of a table and its caption.\n\\textbf{Do not override the default caption sizes.}\n\n\\begin{table}\n  \\centering\n  \\begin{tabular}{lc}\n    \\hline\n    \\textbf{Command} & \\textbf{Output} \\\\\n    \\hline\n    \\verb|{\\\"a}|     & {\\\"a}           \\\\\n    \\verb|{\\^e}|     & {\\^e}           \\\\\n    \\verb|{\\`i}|     & {\\`i}           \\\\\n    \\verb|{\\.I}|     & {\\.I}           \\\\\n    \\verb|{\\o}|      & {\\o}            \\\\\n    \\verb|{\\'u}|     & {\\'u}           \\\\\n    \\verb|{\\aa}|     & {\\aa}           \\\\\\hline\n  \\end{tabular}\n  \\begin{tabular}{lc}\n    \\hline\n    \\textbf{Command} & \\textbf{Output} \\\\\n    \\hline\n    \\verb|{\\c c}|    & {\\c c}          \\\\\n    \\verb|{\\u g}|    & {\\u g}          \\\\\n    \\verb|{\\l}|      & {\\l}            \\\\\n    \\verb|{\\~n}|     & {\\~n}           \\\\\n    \\verb|{\\H o}|    & {\\H o}          \\\\\n    \\verb|{\\v r}|    & {\\v r}          \\\\\n    \\verb|{\\ss}|     & {\\ss}           \\\\\n    \\hline\n  \\end{tabular}\n  \\caption{Example commands for accented characters, to be used in, \\emph{e.g.}, Bib\\TeX{} entries.}\n  \\label{tab:accents}\n\\end{table}\n\nAs much as possible, fonts in figures should conform\nto the document fonts. See Figure~\\ref{fig:experiments} for an example of a figure and its caption.\n\nUsing the \\verb|graphicx| package graphics files can be included within figure\nenvironment at an appropriate point within the text.\nThe \\verb|graphicx| package supports various optional arguments to control the\nappearance of the figure.\nYou must include it explicitly in the \\LaTeX{} preamble (after the\n\\verb|\\documentclass| declaration and before \\verb|\\begin{document}|) using\n\\verb|\\usepackage{graphicx}|.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{example-image-golden}\n  \\caption{A figure with a caption that runs for more than one line.\n    Example image is usually available through the \\texttt{mwe} package\n    without even mentioning it in the preamble.}\n  \\label{fig:experiments}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\includegraphics[width=0.48\\linewidth]{example-image-a} \\hfill\n  \\includegraphics[width=0.48\\linewidth]{example-image-b}\n  \\caption {A minimal working example to demonstrate how to place\n    two images side-by-side.}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Hyperlinks}\n\nUsers of older versions of \\LaTeX{} may encounter the following error during compilation:\n\\begin{quote}\n\\verb|\\pdfendlink| ended up in different nesting level than \\verb|\\pdfstartlink|.\n\\end{quote}\nThis happens when pdf\\LaTeX{} is used and a citation splits across a page boundary. The best way to fix this is to upgrade \\LaTeX{} to 2018-12-01 or later.\n\n\\subsection{Citations}\n\n\\begin{table*}\n  \\centering\n  \\begin{tabular}{lll}\n    \\hline\n    \\textbf{Output}           & \\textbf{natbib command} & \\textbf{ACL only command} \\\\\n    \\hline\n    \\citep{Gusfield:97}       & \\verb|\\citep|           &                           \\\\\n    \\citealp{Gusfield:97}     & \\verb|\\citealp|         &                           \\\\\n    \\citet{Gusfield:97}       & \\verb|\\citet|           &                           \\\\\n    \\citeyearpar{Gusfield:97} & \\verb|\\citeyearpar|     &                           \\\\\n    \\citeposs{Gusfield:97}    &                         & \\verb|\\citeposs|          \\\\\n    \\hline\n  \\end{tabular}\n  \\caption{\\label{citation-guide}\n    Citation commands supported by the style file.\n    The style is based on the natbib package and supports all natbib citation commands.\n    It also supports commands defined in previous ACL style files for compatibility.\n  }\n\\end{table*}\n\nTable~\\ref{citation-guide} shows the syntax supported by the style files.\nWe encourage you to use the natbib styles.\nYou can use the command \\verb|\\citet| (cite in text) to get ``author (year)'' citations, like this citation to a paper by \\citet{Gusfield:97}.\nYou can use the command \\verb|\\citep| (cite in parentheses) to get ``(author, year)'' citations \\citep{Gusfield:97}.\nYou can use the command \\verb|\\citealp| (alternative cite without parentheses) to get ``author, year'' citations, which is useful for using citations within parentheses (e.g. \\citealp{Gusfield:97}).\n\nA possessive citation can be made with the command \\verb|\\citeposs|.\nThis is not a standard natbib command, so it is generally not compatible\nwith other style files.\n\n\\subsection{References}\n\n\\nocite{Ando2005,andrew2007scalable,rasooli-tetrault-2015}\n\nThe \\LaTeX{} and Bib\\TeX{} style files provided roughly follow the American Psychological Association format.\nIf your own bib file is named \\texttt{custom.bib}, then placing the following before any appendices in your \\LaTeX{} file will generate the references section for you:\n\\begin{quote}\n\\begin{verbatim}\n\\bibliography{custom}\n\\end{verbatim}\n\\end{quote}\n\nYou can obtain the complete ACL Anthology as a Bib\\TeX{} file from \\url{https://aclweb.org/anthology/anthology.bib.gz}.\nTo include both the Anthology and your own .bib file, use the following instead of the above.\n\\begin{quote}\n\\begin{verbatim}\n\\bibliography{anthology,custom}\n\\end{verbatim}\n\\end{quote}\n\nPlease see Section~\\ref{sec:bibtex} for information on preparing Bib\\TeX{} files.\n\n\\subsection{Equations}\n\nAn example equation is shown below:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\label{eq:example}\n  A = \\pi r^2\n\\end{equation}\n\nLabels for equation numbers, sections, subsections, figures and tables\nare all defined with the \\verb|\\label{label}| command and cross references\nto them are made with the \\verb|\\ref{label}| command.\n\nThis an example cross-reference to Equation~\\ref{eq:example}.\n\n\\subsection{Appendices}\n\nUse \\verb|\\appendix| before any appendix section to switch the section numbering over to letters. See Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix} for an example.\n\n\\section{Bib\\TeX{} Files}\n\\label{sec:bibtex}\n\nUnicode cannot be used in Bib\\TeX{} entries, and some ways of typing special characters can disrupt Bib\\TeX's alphabetization. The recommended way of typing special characters is shown in Table~\\ref{tab:accents}.\n\nPlease ensure that Bib\\TeX{} records contain DOIs or URLs when possible, and for all the ACL materials that you reference.\nUse the \\verb|doi| field for DOIs and the \\verb|url| field for URLs.\nIf a Bib\\TeX{} entry has a URL or DOI field, the paper title in the references section will appear as a hyperlink to the paper, using the hyperref \\LaTeX{} package.\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nThis document does not cover the content requirements for ACL or any\nother specific venue.  Check the author instructions for\ninformation on\nmaximum page lengths, the required ``Limitations'' section,\nand so on.\n\n\\section*{Acknowledgments}\n\nThis document has been adapted\nby Steven Bethard, Ryan Cotterell and Rui Yan\nfrom the instructions for earlier ACL and NAACL proceedings, including those for\nACL 2019 by Douwe Kiela and Ivan Vuli\\'{c},\nNAACL 2019 by Stephanie Lukin and Alla Roskovskaya,\nACL 2018 by Shay Cohen, Kevin Gimpel, and Wei Lu,\nNAACL 2018 by Margaret Mitchell and Stephanie Lukin,\nBib\\TeX{} suggestions for (NA)ACL 2017/2018 from Jason Eisner,\nACL 2017 by Dan Gildea and Min-Yen Kan,\nNAACL 2017 by Margaret Mitchell,\nACL 2012 by Maggie Li and Michael White,\nACL 2010 by Jing-Shin Chang and Philipp Koehn,\nACL 2008 by Johanna D. Thompson and Donald Walker.\nAdditional elements were taken from the formatting instructions of the \\emph{International Joint Conference on Artificial Intelligence} and the \\emph{Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition}.\n\n% Bibliography entries for the entire Anthology, followed by custom entries\n%\\bibliography{custom,anthology-overleaf-1,anthology-overleaf-2}\n\n% Custom bibliography entries only\n\\bibliography{custom}\n\n\\appendix\n\n\\section{Example Appendix}\n\\label{sec:appendix}\n\nThis is an appendix.\n\n\\end{document}\n",

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "380945e0-78cf-4f7b-8909-30856bc98794",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/figures/gen_fig_cross_arch_heatmap.py",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/figures/gen_fig_cross_arch_heatmap.py",
        "content": "\"\"\"\nCross-architecture detection heatmap: PermG fwd detection across model families.\nnarrative_intent: \"The canary mechanism generalizes across architectures,\nwith interpretable failure modes in specific conditions.\"\n\"\"\"\nimport matplotlib\nmatplotlib.use('Agg')\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\nimport csv\n\nplt.rcParams.update({\n    'font.family': 'DejaVu Sans',\n    'font.size': 9,\n    'axes.titlesize': 10,\n    'axes.labelsize': 9,\n    'xtick.labelsize': 8,\n    'ytick.labelsize': 8,\n    'legend.fontsize': 7,\n    'figure.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.bbox': 'tight',\n    'savefig.pad_inches': 0.03,\n    'axes.spines.top': False,\n    'axes.spines.right': False,\n    'pdf.fonttype': 42,\n    'ps.fonttype': 42,\n})\n\nrows = []\nwith open('artifacts/cross_arch_table7.csv') as f:\n    reader = csv.DictReader(f)\n    for row in reader:\n        if row['PermG_fwd'] and row['note'] not in ('RUNNING', 'RUNNING (~50h)', 'FAILED EXIT_CODE=1'):\n            rows.append(row)\n\ndef parse_frac(s):\n    if not s:\n        return np.nan\n    num, den = s.split('/')\n    return int(num) / int(den)\n\nmodels = []\npermg_fwd = []\nk3_vals = []\nlabels = []\n\nfor r in rows:\n    model = r['model']\n    seed = r['seed']\n    domain = r['domain']\n    prec = r['precision']\n    suffix = ''\n    if domain == 'C4':\n        suffix = ' (C4)'\n    elif prec == 'bf16':\n        suffix = ' (bf16)'\n    label = f\"{model} {seed}{suffix}\"\n    labels.append(label)\n    permg_fwd.append(parse_frac(r['PermG_fwd']))\n    k3_vals.append(parse_frac(r['K3_3sigma']))\n\nn = len(labels)\ny = np.arange(n)\n\nfig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(4.5, 3.2))\n\nbar_height = 0.35\nbars1 = ax.barh(y + bar_height/2, [v*100 for v in permg_fwd], bar_height,\n                color='#4A90C4', alpha=0.9, label='PermG fwd', edgecolor='#222222', linewidth=0.5)\nbars2 = ax.barh(y - bar_height/2, [v*100 for v in k3_vals], bar_height,\n                color='#B8D4E8', alpha=0.75, label=r'$K{\\geq}3$ consensus')\n\n# Value labels\nfor i, (pv, kv) in enumerate(zip(permg_fwd, k3_vals)):\n    ax.text(pv*100 + 1.5, i + bar_height/2, f'{int(pv*8)}/8',\n            va='center', ha='left', fontsize=6, color='#4A90C4', fontweight='bold')\n    ax.text(kv*100 + 1.5, i - bar_height/2, f'{int(kv*8)}/8',\n            va='center', ha='left', fontsize=6, color='#7BBBD6')\n\nax.set_yticks(y)\nax.set_yticklabels(labels, fontsize=7)\nax.set_xlabel('Detection rate (%)')\nax.set_xlim(0, 115)\nax.set_xticks([0, 25, 50, 75, 100])\nax.invert_yaxis()\n\nax.xaxis.grid(True, alpha=0.15, linewidth=0.4)\nax.set_axisbelow(True)\n\n# 75% reference\nax.axvline(x=75, color='#4A90C4', linewidth=0.5, linestyle=':', alpha=0.4)\n\nleg = ax.legend(loc='lower center', bbox_to_anchor=(0.5, 1.02),\n                ncol=2, frameon=True, framealpha=0.9,\n                edgecolor='#cccccc', borderpad=0.3)\nleg.get_frame().set_linewidth(0.4)\n\nfig.subplots_adjust(top=0.92)\nplt.savefig('docs/paper/figures/fig_cross_arch_heatmap.pdf')\nplt.savefig('docs/paper/figures/fig_cross_arch_heatmap.png')\nprint('Saved fig_cross_arch_heatmap.pdf + .png')\n",
        "numLines": 103,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 103
      }
    },
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "e156b13c-0e4c-4ecd-abee-2e78bf01cadf",

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\subsection{Model Collapse: Theory and Empirical Evidence}\n\\label{sec:rw_collapse}\n\nIterative training collapse was formalized by \\citet{shumailov2024curse} and \\citet{alemohammad2024mad}; \\citet{schaeffer2025position} systematize eight distinct notions of collapse; our operationalization aligns with their \\emph{distributional shift} family (definitions~D4--D6).\nTheoretical analyses sharpen these results: \\citet{dohmatob2024tale} recast collapse as changed scaling-law exponents, \\citet{dohmatob2025strong} proved collapse at vanishingly small synthetic fractions, and \\citet{truong2026phase} prove entropy collapse is a first-order transition, structurally ruling out critical-slowing-down early-warning signals~\\citep{scheffer2009early,scheffer2012anticipating}.\nEmpirically, \\citet{hataya2023will} demonstrate that generated images degrade downstream classification on ImageNet-scale data, and \\citet{kovac2025recursive} show that training data properties (lexical diversity, semantic diversity) modulate the magnitude of recursive collapse in LLMs.\nCritical mixture ratios separating qualitative outcomes have independent precedent: \\citet{kazdan2025collapse} map collapse conditions, \\citet{gu2024cmr} establish scaling laws for critical ratios, and \\citet{gu2025datamixing} show data mixing induces phase transitions in knowledge acquisition.\n\n\\subsection{Detection of Synthetic Data Contamination}\n\\label{sec:rw_detection}\n\nMonitoring metrics proposed for collapse include expected calibration error (ECE)~\\citep{du2025scales}, surplexity~\\citep{gambetta2024surplexity}, and machine-generated text detection~\\citep{drayson2025detection}; \\citet{shi2025closer} independently validate entropy as a collapse diagnostic in diffusion models; \\citet{buffa2026entropy} propose continuous entropy-based monitoring for LLM accuracy, though targeting task performance rather than distributional collapse.\nA complementary line of work pursues \\emph{proactive} contamination prevention via watermarking~\\citep{kirchenbauer2023watermark}; our distributional detection framework is \\emph{reactive}, identifying collapse from observed metric trajectories without requiring control over the generation process.\n\\citet{boettiger2012limits} establish fundamental power limits for early-warning detection in short time series ($T{<}25$), directly relevant to iterative training where observation windows are typically $T{=}5$--$20$.\nHowever, these works evaluate single metrics in isolation without cross-validating against alternative statistical tests.\nWe differ by applying five detection methods to identical data, mapping detectability across contamination regimes, and treating method sensitivity as a primary finding rather than a robustness check (detailed comparison: Appendix~\\ref{app:method_comparison}).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Methods for Distribution Shift}\n\\label{sec:rw_methods}\n\nFor temporal precedence testing, Granger causality~\\citep{granger1969investigating} is the standard framework, with neural extensions~\\citep{tank2021neural,runge2019detecting}; however, non-stationary series inflate rejection rates~\\citep{granger1974spurious}, and short series ($T{\\leq}25$) preclude reliable nonlinear estimation~\\citep{boettiger2012limits}.\nGranger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not true causation: bivariate tests cannot exclude confounders~\\citep{shojaie2022granger}.\nOur permutation-based approach circumvents the critical-slowing-down limitation by detecting temporal predictive improvement rather than variance or autocorrelation signatures.\nSpecification-curve analysis~\\citep{simonsohn2020specification} formalizes the sensitivity of conclusions to analytical choices; we adapt this framework to show that method choice alone shifts detection rates from 0\\% to 94\\%.\nThe multiverse analysis perspective~\\citep{steegen2016increasing,silberzahn2018many,coretta2023multidimensional,kapoor2024reforms} motivates our multi-method comparison: five methods on identical data produce conclusions ranging from universal significance to complete null, instantiating many-analysts divergence at the method level.\n",
        "numLines": 29,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 29
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/related_work.tex"
```
